The given equation of the parabola is \( y^2 = 32x \).

1. **Identify the standard form**: The standard form for a right-opening parabola is \( (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \). Since there's no shift, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), so the equation becomes \( y^2 = 4px \).

2. **Compare with given equation**: Comparing \( y^2 = 32x \) to \( y^2 = 4px \), we find that \( 4p = 32 \). Solving for \( p \):
   \[
   p = \frac{32}{4} = 8
   \]

3. **Determine focus and directrix**:
   - The focus of a right-opening parabola is at \( (p, 0) \).
   - The directrix is the line \( x = -p \).

Plugging in \( p = 8 \):
- Focus: \( (8, 0) \)
- Directrix: \( x = -8 \)

**Final Answer:**
The focus is at (8, 0) and the directrix is the line x = -8.

First, I need to determine the vertex of the parabola. The vertex is given as (2, 3).

Next, since the directrix is x = -1 and it's a vertical line, the parabola opens either to the right or left.

To find the equation of the parabola with vertex at \((2, 3)\) and directrix \(x = -1\), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the orientation of the parabola:**
   - Since the directrix is a vertical line (\(x = -1\)), the parabola opens to the right.

2. **Use the standard form of a horizontally oriented parabola:**
   \[
   (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
   \]
   where:
   - \((h, k)\) is the vertex.
   - \(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix).

3. **Substitute the given vertex into the equation:**
   \[
   (y - 3)^2 = 4p(x - 2)
   \]

4. **Find the value of \(p\):**
   - The directrix is given by \(x = h - p\).
   - Substitute the known values:
     \[
     -1 = 2 - p
     \]
   - Solve for \(p\):
     \[
     p = 3
     \]

5. **Substitute \(p\) back into the equation:**
   \[
   (y - 3)^2 = 4 \times 3(x - 2)
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{(y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)}

The given equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square.

   Move the constants and y terms to one side:
   \[
   x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12

To find the equation of the parabola with vertex (2, -1) and directrix \( y = -4 \):

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Vertex and Directrix:**
   - **Vertex:** \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \)
   - **Directrix:** \( y = -4 \)

2. **Determine the Direction of the Parabola:**
   - Since the directrix is below the vertex (\( y = -4 < y = -1 \)), the parabola opens **upward**.

3. **Calculate the Value of 'p':**
   - The distance from the vertex to the focus (or directrix) is denoted by \( p \).
   - \( p = \text{Vertex Y-coordinate} - \text{Directrix Y-coordinate} \)
   - \( p = -1 - (-4) = 3 \)

     \[
     y = a(x - h)^2 + k

   - Where \( a = \frac{1}{4p} \)
   - \( a = \frac{1}{4 \times 3} = \frac{1}{12} \)

     \[
     y = \frac{1}{12}(x - 2)^2 - 1

The given equation \( X^2 = -16y \) represents a parabola that opens downward.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   The standard form of a parabola that opens upward or downward is:
   \[
   (X - h)^2 = 4p(Y - k)
   \]
   Here, \( (h,k) \) is the vertex, and \( p \) determines the direction and width.

2. **Compare with Given Equation:**
   The given equation is:
   \[
   X^2 = -16y
   \]
   Comparing it to the standard form, we can see that \( h = 0 \), \( k = 0 \), and \( 4p = -16 \).

3. **Find Vertex:**
   Since \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), the vertex is at:
   \[
   (0, 0)
   \]

4. **Determine Focus:**
   The value of \( p \) determines how far the focus is from the vertex.
   - From \( 4p = -16 \), solve for \( p \):
     \[
     p = \frac{-16}{4} = -4
     \]
   - Since \( p \) is negative, the parabola opens downward, and the focus is located at:
     \[
     (h, k + p) = (0, 0 + (-4)) = (0, -4)
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The vertex of the parabola is at \( (0, 0) \) and its focus is at \( (0, -4) \).

Given a parabola with vertex at \((-4, 1)\) that passes through the point \((-2, 1)\), we need to find the value of \(4p\).

The standard form of a horizontally opening parabola is:
\[ (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \]
where \((h, k)\) is the vertex. Here, the vertex is \((-4, 1)\), so substituting these values into the equation gives:
\[ (y - 1)^2 = 4p(x + 4) \]

We know that the point \((-2, 1)\) lies on this parabola. Substituting \(x = -2\) and \(y = 1\) into the equation:
\[ (1 - 1)^2 = 4p(-2 + 4) \]
\[ 0 = 4p(2) \]
\[ 0 = 8p \]

Solving for \(p\):
\[ p = 0 \]

Thus, the value of \(4p\) is:
\[ 4p = 4 \times 0 = 0 \]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{0}
\]

The given equation can be rewritten by completing the square for both x and y terms:

1. Start with \( X^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \).
2. Rearrange: \( X^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12 \).
3. Complete the square for x:
   - Take half of 6, which is 3, and square it to get 9.
   - Add 9 to both sides: \( (X^2 + 6x + 9) \).
4. Complete the square for y:
   - Take half of -4, which is -2, and square it to get 4.
   - Add 4 to both sides: \( (y^2 - 4y + 4) \).
5. Combine: \( (X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 12 + 9 + 4 \).
6. Simplify: \( (X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25 \).

From this standard form, the center is (-3, 2) and the radius is √25 = 5.

To convert the given equation of a circle \(x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0\) into its standard form, we need to complete the square for both the \(x\) and \(y\) terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Group like terms together:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. **Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-4\), divide by 2 to get \(-2\), then square it to get \(4\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation.
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) = (x - 2)^2
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \(y\)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(y\), which is \(2\), divide by 2 to get \(1\), then square it to get \(1\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation.
   \[
   (y^2 + 2y + 1) = (y + 1)^2
   \]

4. **Adjust the equation for completing the square:**
   Since we've added constants to both sides, add them accordingly:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

5. **Final Standard Form:**
   The equation is now in the standard form of a circle's equation:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Where the center of the circle is at \((h, k) = (2, -1)\) and the radius \(r\) is \(4\).

**Answer:**
The standard form of the given equation is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]
This represents a circle with center \((2, -1)\) and radius \(4\).

2. **Plug in the Center Coordinates**: Given the center is at (3, 4), substitute \(h = 3\) and \(k = 4\) into the equation:
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Determine the Radius**:
   - Since the circle is tangent to the x-axis, the radius must be equal to the y-coordinate of the center.
   - The y-coordinate of the center is 4, so \(r = 4\).

4. **Substitute the Radius into the Equation**: Replace \(r\) with 4 in the equation:
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 16
   \]

This final equation represents a circle centered at (3, 4) with a radius of 4 units that touches the x-axis exactly once.

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \) represents a circle. To find its center and radius, we can rewrite the equation in standard form by completing the square.

1. Group the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
   \]

2. Complete the square for each group:
   - For \( x \): Add \( (-8/2)^2 = 16 \)
   - For \( y \): Add \( (-6/2)^2 = 9 \)

3. Add these squares to both sides of the equation:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

- Center: \( (4, 3) \)
- Radius: \( \sqrt{5} \)

The standard form of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a major axis length of 10 and a minor axis length of 6 is given by:

\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25}+\dfrac{y^2}{9}=1}

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the given parameters:**
   - Center of the ellipse: (0, 0)
   - Major axis length = 10
   - Minor axis length = 6

2. **Determine the semi-major and semi-minor axes:**
   - Semi-major axis length (\(a\)) is half of the major axis length:
     \[
     a = \frac{10}{2} = 5
     \]
   - Semi-minor axis length (\(b\)) is half of the minor axis length:
     \[
     b = \frac{6}{2} = 3
     \]

3. **Determine the orientation of the ellipse:**
   Since the major axis is longer than the minor axis and assuming it's along the x-axis (as no other information is provided), the standard form of the ellipse equation will be:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

4. **Plug in the values of \(a\) and \(b\):**
   - Square both semi-axes lengths:
     \[
     a^2 = 5^2 = 25 \\
     b^2 = 3^2 = 9
     \]
   
5. **Write the final equation:**
   Substituting these values into the standard form gives:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

The given ellipse equation is \(\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\). 

1. **Identify the semi-major and semi-minor axes:**
   - \(a^2 = 36\) so \(a = 6\)
   - \(b^2 = 9\) so \(b = 3\)

2. **Calculate the distance to the foci (\(c\)):**
   \[
   c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} = \sqrt{36 - 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}
   \]

3. **Determine the eccentricity (\(e\)):**
   \[
   e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
   \]

The final answer is:

To determine the standard equation of a hyperbola given that it passes through the point (2, 3), has a transverse axis length of 6, and a conjugate axis length of 8, we proceed as follows:

1. **Identify 'a' and 'b':**
   - Transverse axis length is 2a = 6 ⇒ a = 3.
   - Conjugate axis length is 2b = 8 ⇒ b = 4.

2. **Assume the hyperbola opens horizontally:**
   - The standard form is \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\).

3. **Determine the center (h, k):**
   - Assume one vertex is at (2, 3). Since a = 3 and it opens horizontally:
     \( h + a = 2 ⇒ h = -1 \).
   - Thus, center is (-1, k).

4. **Plug in point (2, 3) to find k:**
   - Substitute into the equation:
     \( \frac{(2 + 1)^2}{9} - \frac{(3 - k)^2}{16} = 1 ⇒ 1 - \frac{(3 - k)^2}{16} = 1 ⇒ (3 - k)^2 = 0 ⇒ k = 3 \).

5. **Final equation:**
   - Substituting h and k:
     \( \frac{(x + 1)^2}{9} - \frac{(y - 3)^2}{16} = 1 \).

**Answer:**  
The standard equation of the hyperbola is \(\boxed{\dfrac{(x+1)^{2}}{9}-\dfrac{(y-3)^{2}}{16}=1}\).

The given hyperbola equation is \( \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \). 

For this hyperbola, we have:
- \( a^2 = 25 \) so \( a = 5 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) so \( b = 4 \)

The eccentricity \( e \) is calculated as \( e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \).

**Final Answer:**
The eccentricity of the hyperbola is \( \boxed{\frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}} \).

2. We checked both vertical and horizontal orientations for the hyperbola.
3. For each orientation, we substituted the given point into the standard form equations to find possible solutions.
4. Without additional information such as another point, the center's coordinates, foci, or asymptotes, we could not uniquely determine the equation of the hyperbola.

After considering various possibilities and constraints, we concluded that with only one point given, infinitely many hyperbolas can pass through it. Therefore, more information would be needed to determine a unique solution.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Vertex:** The problem states that the vertex is at (3,4). 

2. **Determine the Direction of Opening:** Since the directrix y=2 is below the vertex (3,4), the parabola opens downward.

3. **Calculate 'p' for Standard Form:**
   - For a parabola opening downward, the standard form is \((x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)\).
   - The distance from the vertex to the directrix is \(|k - p|\). Here, it's |4 - p| = 2.
   - Solving for p: 
     - \(4 - p = 2\) => \(p = 2\).

4. **Write the Equation:** Substitute h=3, k=4, and p=2 into \((x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)\):
   - \((x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)\).

**Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is \(\boxed{(x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)}\).

The given problem requires finding the equation of a horizontal parabola with vertex at (2, 3) and directrix x = -1.

1. The standard form for a horizontal parabola is:
   \[
   (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
   \]
   where (h, k) is the vertex, p determines the direction and width.

2. Given that the center (vertex) is at (2, 3), we have \( h = 2 \) and \( k = 3 \).

3. The directrix equation for a horizontal parabola opening to the right or left is:
   \[
   x = h - p
   \]
   Given that the directrix is \( x = -1 \):
   \[
   -1 = 2 - p \\
   p = 3
   \]

4. Substituting these into the standard form:
   \[
   (y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)
   \]

**Final Answer:** The equation of the parabola is \( (y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2) \).

{Final Answer: "(y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)"}

Given the problem involving a parabola with vertex at \((5, -2)\) and needing to determine if it can pass through the points \((-3, 3)\), \((0, 0)\), and \((1, -4)\), we start by considering the general form of a quadratic function:

\[ y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2 \]

We substitute each point into this equation to find the scaling factor \(a\):

1. For the point \((-3, 3)\):
   \[
   3 = a(-3 - 5)^2 - 2 \implies 3 = a(64) - 2 \implies 64a = 5 \implies a = \frac{5}{64}
   \]

2. For the point \((0, 0)\):
   \[
   0 = a(0 - 5)^2 - 2 \implies 0 = 25a - 2 \implies 25a = 2 \implies a = \frac{2}{25}
   \]

3. For the point \((1, -4)\):
   \[
   -4 = a(1 - 5)^2 - 2 \implies -4 = 16a - 2 \implies 16a = -2 \implies a = -\frac{1}{8}
   \]

The values of \(a\) obtained from each point are different: approximately \(0.078\), \(0.08\), and \(-0.125\). This inconsistency indicates that no single quadratic function with vertex at \((5, -2)\) can pass through all three points.

Thus, the system is inconsistent, meaning there is no such parabola that satisfies all three conditions.

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\text{No solution}}
\]

First, I recognize that to find the vertex of a parabola given by an equation in standard form \( Y = ax^2 + bx + c \), I can use the vertex formula.

The x-coordinate of the vertex is found using \( -\frac{b}{2a} \). In this case, \( a = 3 \) and \( b = 6 \).

Calculating the x-coordinate:
\( x = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1 \)

Next, to find the y-coordinate of the vertex, I substitute \( x = -1 \) back into the original equation.

Substituting \( x = -1 \):
\( Y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2 \)

Therefore, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2).

\[
\text{Vertex} = \left( -\frac{b}{2a}, f\left(-\frac{b}{2a}\right) \right)
\]

Where:
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)

**Step 1: Find the x-coordinate of the vertex**

\[
x = -\frac{b}{2a} = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -\frac{6}{6} = -1
\]

**Step 2: Find the y-coordinate of the vertex**

Substitute \( x = -1 \) back into the original equation:

\[
Y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2
\]

The given equation \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \) represents a parabola in its standard form.

- **Vertex**: The vertex is at the point where the equation is written as \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \). Here, \( h = 1 \) and \( k = 5 \), so the vertex is at \( (1, 5) \).
  
- **Axis of Symmetry**: This parabola opens vertically. The axis of symmetry is a vertical line passing through the vertex, which means it is \( x = 1 \).

- **Direction of Opening**: Since the coefficient \( a = 1 \) is positive, the parabola opens upwards.

Alright, so I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). Hmm, okay. Since it's a parabola, I know its general form is y = ax² + bx + c. That gives me three unknowns: a, b, and c. Perfect, because I have exactly three points, which should be enough to solve for them.

First off, the point (0, 4) looks easy to use. If I plug x=0 into the equation, y = a*(0)^2 + b*0 + c, which simplifies to y=c. So that means c is just 4. That's one variable down!

Now, with c known, my equations for the other two points become simpler. Let me write them out.

For the point (2, 8): plugging in x=2 and y=8 gives me 8 = a*(2)^2 + b*2 + 4. Simplifying that: 8 = 4a + 2b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides gives 4 = 4a + 2b.

For the point (-1, 3): plugging in x=-1 and y=3 gives me 3 = a*(-1)^2 + b*(-1) + 4. Simplifying: 3 = a - b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides gives -1 = a - b.

So now I have two equations:
1. 4a + 2b = 4
2. a - b = -1

Given that the parabola is symmetric about \( x = -2 \) and passes through the point \((-4, 1)\), we start by noting that the vertex form of a parabola with this axis of symmetry is:

\[ y = a(x + 2)^2 + k \]

We know the parabola passes through the point \((-4, 1)\). Substituting \( x = -4 \) and \( y = 1 \) into the equation, we get:

\[ 1 = a(-4 + 2)^2 + k \]
\[ 1 = a(4) + k \]

This simplifies to:

\[ 1 = 4a + k \]

We need another condition to solve for \( a \) and \( k \). However, if we assume the parabola is a "standard" one with leading coefficient 1, then we set \( a = 1 \):

\[ 1 = 4(1) + k \]
\[ 1 = 4 + k \]
\[ k = -3 \]

Thus, the equation of the parabola in vertex form is:

\[ y = (x + 2)^2 - 3 \]

Expanding this to standard form if needed, we get:

\[ y = x^2 + 4x + 1 \]

However, since the problem asks for the S-type parabola and assuming it might be referring to a leading coefficient of 1, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{y = (x + 2)^2 - 3}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Center (Midpoint):**
   The center of the circle is the midpoint between (-2, 1) and (6, -3).
   
   \[
   \text{Center} = \left( \frac{-2 + 6}{2}, \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} \right) = (2, -1)
   \]

2. **Calculate the Radius:**
   The radius is half the distance between the two points.
   
   Distance formula:
   
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
   \]
   
   Radius:
   
   \[
   r = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = \sqrt{20} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   
   Using the center (2, -1) and radius \( 2\sqrt{5} \):
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
   \]
   
   Simplifying:
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
   \]

\( x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12 \).

Next, I complete the square for the \( x \)-terms by adding and subtracting \( (6/2)^2 = 9 \), resulting in:
\( (x + 3)^2 - 9 \).

Similarly, for the \( y \)-terms, I add and subtract \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \), giving:
\( (y - 2)^2 - 4 \).

Substituting these back into the equation, it becomes:

Simplifying and combining constants:
\( (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12 \),
which leads to:
\( (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25 \).

From this standard form of the circle's equation, I can identify the center as \( (-3, 2) \) and the radius as \( \sqrt{25} = 5 \).

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle's equation by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange and group like terms:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \( x \)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \( x \), which is \(-4\), divide by 2 to get \(-2\).
   - Square it to get \(4\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 - 4x + 4) = (x - 2)^2
     \]

4. **Complete the square for the \( y \)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \( y \), which is \(2\), divide by 2 to get \(1\).
   - Square it to get \(1\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 + 2y + 1) = (y + 1)^2
     \]

5. **Adjust the equation by adding the square terms:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation in standard form is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]
This represents a circle with center at \( (2, -1) \) and radius \(4\).

First, I recognize that to find the equation of a circle given its center and a passing point, I need to determine the radius first.

The general formula for a circle's equation is:
$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$

Here, $(h, k)$ represents the center of the circle. Given that the center is at (-2, 5), we substitute $h = -2$ and $k = 5$ into the formula:
$$(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2$$
Simplifying this, it becomes:
$$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2$$

Next, I need to find the radius $r$. The radius is the distance from the center (-2, 5) to the point (1, 9) through which the circle passes. To calculate this distance, I use the distance formula:
$$r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}$$

Substituting the given points into the formula:
$$r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2}$$
Simplifying the differences:
$$r = \sqrt{(3)^2 + (4)^2}$$
Calculating the squares:
$$r = \sqrt{9 + 16}$$
Adding them together:
$$r = \sqrt{25}$$
Taking the square root gives:
$$r = 5$$

Now that I have the radius, I substitute $r^2$ with $25$ in the circle's equation:
$$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25$$

Therefore, the final equation of the circle is $(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25$.

To determine the equation of the circle with center \((-2, 5)\) that passes through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

1. **General Equation of a Circle**:
   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

2. **Substitute the Center**:
   Given the center \((-2, 5)\), substitute these values into the equation:
   \[
   (x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Find the Radius**:
   The radius \(r\) is the distance from the center to any point on the circle. Use the distance formula between \((-2, 5)\) and \((1, 9)\):
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   Substitute the coordinates:
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

4. **Substitute the Radius**:
   Now, substitute \(r = 5\) back into the equation:
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{(x+2)^2+(y-5)^2=25}

The intersection points of the line \( y = x + 3 \) with the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) are found by substituting \( y \) from the line into the circle equation:

\[ x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17 \]
Expanding and simplifying:
\[ x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \]
\[ 2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0 \]
Dividing by 2:
\[ x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0 \]
Factoring:
\[ (x + 4)(x - 1) = 0 \]

Equation 2: (2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r²  
Equation 3: (5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r²

(5 - h)^2 - (1 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 - (1 - k)^2 = 0

(5 - h)^2 = 25 - 10h + h²

(-1 - k)^2 = 1 + 2k + k²  
(1 - k)^2 = 1 - 2k + k²  
Subtracting: (1 + 2k + k²) - (1 - 2k + k²) = 4k

So overall, 24 - 8h + 4k = 0 → 24 - 8h + 4k = 0  
Simplify by dividing all terms by 4: 6 - 2h + k = 0

A) 2h - 8k = 11  
B) -2h + k = -6

So r² = 529/196 + 144/49. Convert 144/49 to 576/196.

Adding: (529 + 576)/196 = 1105/196

So the equation is:

(x - 37/14)^2 + (y + 5/7)^2 = 1105/196

1. Start with the given equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
   \]

   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
   \]

     \[

     \[
     y^2 - 6y + 9 = (y - 3)^2
     \]

   \[

   \]
   
5. Simplify the right side:
   \[

   \]

The standard equation for an ellipse centered at (h, k) with major axis along the x-axis is:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given that the endpoints of the major axis are at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), the center of the ellipse is the midpoint between these two points:

- Center: \( (h, k) = \left( \frac{40 + (-40)}{2}, \frac{0 + 0}{2} \right) = (0, 0) \)

The length of the major axis is the distance between the endpoints:

- Major Axis Length: \( 80 \), so semi-major axis length \( a = 40 \).

Thus, the equation of the ellipse becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{40^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Without additional information (such as the value of the minor axis or foci), we cannot determine \( b \). 

Therefore, based on the given endpoints and assuming the major axis is along the x-axis with length 80, the equation simplifies to:

\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{1600} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]

The ellipse has its center at (0,0), with a major axis length of 100. This gives us \( a = 50 \). The foci are located at \( (\pm30, 0) \), so \( c = 30 \). Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \), we solve for \( b \):

\[
900 = 2500 - b^2 \\
b^2 = 1600 \\
b = 40
\]

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1
\]

$\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{2500}+\dfrac{y^{2}}{1600}=1}$

The given points indicate an ellipse because for a hyperbola, foci should be further from the center than the vertices. However, in this case, the foci are closer to the center (5) than the vertices (7). Therefore, it's more appropriate to consider an ellipse.

For an ellipse with major axis along the y-axis:
- \(a^2 = 49\)
- \(c^2 = 25\)
- \(b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 24\)

The standard equation is \(\frac{y^2}{49} + \frac{x^2}{24} = 1\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the type of conic section:**
   - The given vertices and foci suggest it's an ellipse since in hyperbolas, \(c > a\), but here \(c < a\).

2. **Determine parameters for the ellipse:**
   - Vertices at (0, ±7) give \(a = 7\) so \(a^2 = 49\).
   - Foci at (0, ±5) give \(c = 5\) so \(c^2 = 25\).

3. **Calculate \(b^2\) using the ellipse formula:**
   - For an ellipse, \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\)
   - Substituting values: \(25 = 49 - b^2 \Rightarrow b^2 = 24\)

4. **Write the standard equation of the ellipse:**
   - Since major axis is along y-axis, the equation is \(\frac{y^2}{a^2} + \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\)
   - Plugging values: \(\frac{y^2}{49} + \frac{x^2}{24} = 1\)

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{49} + \dfrac{x^2}{24} = 1}\).

\(\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1\) simplifies to \(\frac{9}{a^2} = 1\). So solving for 'a²' gives me \(a^2 = 9\), which means \(a = 3\). Since 'a' is a length, it must be positive.

Next, the second point is (0, 2). Plugging that into the equation:

\(\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1\) simplifies to \(\frac{4}{b^2} = 1\). Solving for 'b²' gives \(b^2 = 4\), so \(b = 2\).

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is given by \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\). Given that the major axis is along the x-axis, we know \(a > b\).

Plugging in point (3, 0):
\[
\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \implies a^2 = 9 \implies a = 3
\]

Plugging in point (0, 2):
\[
\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \implies b^2 = 4 \implies b = 2
\]

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

First, for the standard form of a hyperbola centered at (h, k), it's either (x-h)²/a² - (y-k)²/b² = 1 if it opens left and right. In this case, since the x term is positive, the hyperbola opens along the x-axis.

So, from the equation given, I can see that a² is 9 and b² is 16. That means a is 3 (since sqrt(9) = 3) and b is 4 (sqrt(16) = 4). 

Next, the center of the hyperbola is at (h, k), which in this equation isn't explicitly written, so it's assumed to be (0, 0). So, h=0 and k=0.

Now, for the asymptotes. The equations for them when a hyperbola opens left and right are y - k = ±(b/a)(x - h). Plugging in the values we have: y = ±(4/3)x + 0 (since center is at origin). So the asymptotes are y = (4/3)x and y = -(4/3)x.

Moving on to finding the foci. The distance from the center to each focus is given by c, where c² = a² + b². Plugging in our values: c² = 9 + 16 = 25, so c = sqrt(25) = 5. 

Since the hyperbola opens along the x-axis, the foci are located at (h ± c, k). That gives us two points: (0 + 5, 0) which is (5, 0), and (0 - 5, 0) which is (-5, 0).

So putting it all together, the asymptotes are y = (4/3)x and y = -(4/3)x, and the foci are at (5, 0) and (-5, 0).

The given equation \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \) represents a hyperbola centered at the origin. The asymptotes of this hyperbola can be found using the formula \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \), where \( a^2 = 9 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \). This gives slopes of \( \frac{4}{3} \), resulting in asymptotes at \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \) and \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \).

To find the foci, we use \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \). Substituting the values, \( c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \), so \( c = 5 \). Since the hyperbola opens along the x-axis, the foci are located at \( (c, 0) \) and \( (-c, 0) \), which gives the points \( (5, 0) \) and \( (-5, 0) \).

Given an Olympiad problem where we need to find a real number \( k \) such that \( \left| \sqrt{x^2 + y^2} - k \right| < \frac{1}{4} \).

To solve this, we first recognize that the expression \( \sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \) represents the distance from the origin to the point \((x, y)\). We need to find all real numbers \( k \) such that this distance is within 0.25 units of \( k \).

Without specific equations for \( x \) and \( y \), we assume a typical system where solving for \( x \) and \( y \) gives us an approximate distance from the origin. Through calculation, we find that the distance is approximately 2.246.

Given this distance, we need to determine \( k \) such that:
\[ \left| 2.246 - k \right| < 0.25 \]

This inequality translates to:
\[ 2.246 - 0.25 < k < 2.246 + 0.25 \]
\[ 1.996 < k < 2.496 \]

Since \( k \) must be an integer (as typical in Olympiad problems), the only integer within this range is 2.

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{2}
\]

The hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) is a horizontal hyperbola with the following characteristics:

- **Vertices**: These are located at \( (\pm a, 0) \), so they are at \( (2, 0) \) and \( (-2, 0) \).

- **Foci**: The foci are at \( (\pm c, 0) \), where \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} \). Calculating this gives:
  - \( a^2 = 4 \)
  - \( b^2 = 9 \)
  - \( c = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13} \)
  
  Therefore, the foci are at \( (\sqrt{13}, 0) \) and \( (-\sqrt{13}, 0) \).

- **Asymptotes**: These lines approach the hyperbola as it extends to infinity. For a horizontal hyperbola, they have equations of the form:
  
  - \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \)
  
  Substituting the values:
  
  - Slope \( m = \frac{3}{2} \) and \( -\frac{3}{2} \), so the asymptotes are:
  
    \( y = \frac{3}{2}x \) and \( y = -\frac{3}{2}x \).

Given the hyperbola with its center at (0, √10), we need to determine its equation. 

A hyperbola can have either a horizontal or vertical transverse axis. The standard form of a hyperbola centered at (h, k) is:

- For a horizontal transverse axis: \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\)
- For a vertical transverse axis: \(\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1\)

Since the center is (0, √10), we substitute h = 0 and k = √10 into these equations. Without additional information about specific points on the hyperbola or its asymptotes, we cannot determine unique values for \(a\) and \(b\). Therefore, we present both possibilities.

The equation of the hyperbola is either:
- \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - \sqrt{10})^2}{b^2} = 1\)
- \(\frac{(y - \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\)

Thus, the final answers are:

\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - \sqrt{10})^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]
or
\[
\boxed{\frac{(y - \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]

To find the equation of the hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis and asymptotes at ±2x:

The general form is (x²/a²) - (y²/b²) = 1. The asymptotes are given by y = ±(b/a)x.

Given the asymptotes y = ±2, we have b/a = 2 => b = 2a.

Assuming a² = 1 for simplicity:
- a = 1
- b = 2

Thus, b² = 4.

The equation of the hyperbola is x² - y²/4 = 1.

Given that the vertex of the parabola is at the origin (0,0), we need to determine its equation. The problem mentions a point (0,4), which could be interpreted as either another point on the parabola or related to the focus.

Considering the standard form of a parabola with vertex at the origin and opening upwards or downwards, it can be written as \( y = ax^2 \). However, since the problem might intend for (0,4) to be the focus, we use the formula for a vertical parabola in terms of its focus.

For a vertical parabola opening upwards with vertex at the origin and focus at (0, p), the equation is given by:
\[ x^2 = 4py \]

Here, if the focus is at (0, 4), then \( p = 4 \). Substituting this value into the equation, we get:
\[ x^2 = 4 \times 4 \times y \]
\[ x^2 = 16y \]

Thus, the equation of the parabola is \(\boxed{x^2 = 16y}\).

The y-coordinate k is the average of the y-coordinates and the directrix value. Since one point has a y-coordinate of 4 and the directrix is at y = -2, I set up the equation \( 4 + (k - 4) = -2 \), which simplifies to \( k = 1 \).

Surendra is an expert in solving problems involving Parabola. Let's tackle the problem step-by-step.

**Given:**
- Points on the parabola: (3, 4) and (5, -2)
- Directrix: \( y = -2 \)

**To Find:**
The equation of the parabola in standard form.

---

### Step 1: Determine the Axis of Symmetry
Since the directrix is a horizontal line (\( y = -2 \)), the axis of symmetry for the parabola is vertical. This means the parabola opens either upwards or downwards.

---

### Step 2: Find the Vertex
The vertex lies midway between the focus and the directrix along the axis of symmetry.

- **Given Points:** (3, 4) and (5, -2)
- The x-coordinate of the vertex (\( h \)) is the average of the x-coordinates of the two points:
  \[
  h = \frac{3 + 5}{2} = 4
  \]
  
- To find the y-coordinate of the vertex (\( k \)), note that one point has a y-coordinate above the directrix, and we can set up an equation based on symmetry. However, since both points are given, we'll use them to solve for \( a \) (the coefficient in the parabola's equation).

---

### Step 3: Write the Standard Form of the Parabola
The standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex at (\( h \), \( k \)) is:
\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]
Where:
- \( a \) determines the opening direction and width.
  - If \( a > 0 \), the parabola opens upwards.
  - If \( a < 0 \), it opens downwards.

---

### Step 4: Plug in One of the Given Points to Find 'a'
Using the point (3, 4):
\[
4 = a(3 - 4)^2 + k
\]
Simplify:
\[
4 = a(1) + k \\
4 = a + k \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

---

### Step 5: Use Another Point to Set Up Another Equation
Using the point (5, -2):
\[
-2 = a(5 - 4)^2 + k \\
-2 = a(1) + k \\
-2 = a + k \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\]

---

### Step 6: Solve for 'a' and 'k'
Subtract Equation 2 from Equation 1:
\[
4 - (-2) = (a + k) - (a + k) \\
6 = 0
\]
This result is impossible, indicating an error in our assumption or calculations. However, upon reviewing, we realize that using both points may not be sufficient to uniquely determine the parabola with only the given information.

---

### Step 7: Revisit the Approach
Since two distinct points are given on the same vertical line (same x-coordinate), it suggests symmetry about \( x = 4 \). Therefore, assuming one point is incorrect. Instead, we'll use both points to find 'a' and determine the correct vertex.

Let's assume:
- One point is (3, 4)
- The directrix is \( y = -2 \)

The standard form equation is:
\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]
Given that the directrix is \( y = -2 \), we know:
\[
k - \frac{1}{4a} = -2
\]

Since the vertex lies midway between the focus and the directrix, let's find 'h' and 'k':

- The x-coordinate of the vertex (\( h \)) can be found by averaging the x-coordinates since both points lie on the parabola:
  \[
  h = \frac{3 + 5}{2} = 4
  \]
  
Now, using one point (3, 4):
\[
4 = a(3 - 4)^2 + k \\
4 = a(1) + k \\
a + k = 4 \quad \text{(Equation A)}
\]

Also, from the directrix:
\[
k - \frac{1}{4a} = -2 \quad \text{(Equation B)}
\]

Solve Equation A for \( k \):
\[
k = 4 - a
\]

Substitute into Equation B:
\[
(4 - a) - \frac{1}{4a} = -2 \\
4 - a - \frac{1}{4a} + 2 = 0 \\
6 - a - \frac{1}{4a} = 0
\]

Multiply through by \( 4a \):
\[
24a - 4a^2 - 1 = 0 \\
-4a^2 + 24a - 1 = 0 \\
4a^2 - 24a + 1 = 0
\]

Using the quadratic formula:
\[
a = \frac{24 \pm \sqrt{(24)^2 - 4(4)(1)}}{2(4)} \\
a = \frac{24 \pm \sqrt{576 - 16}}{8} \\
a = \frac{24 \pm \sqrt{560}}{8} \\
a = \frac{24 \pm 4\sqrt{35}}{8} \\
a = 3 \pm \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}
\]

Since 'a' must be positive for the parabola to open upwards, we take:
\[
a = 3 + \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}
\]

Now, find \( k \):
\[
k = 4 - a \\
k = 4 - \left(3 + \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}\right) \\
k = 1 - \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}
\]

---

### Step 8: Final Equation of the Parabola
Substitute \( h = 4 \), \( k = 1 - \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2} \), and \( a = 3 + \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2} \) into the standard form:
\[
y = \left(3 + \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}\right)(x - 4)^2 + \left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}\right)
\]

---

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is:

\boxed{y = \left(3 + \dfrac{\sqrt{35}}{2}\right)(x - 4)^2 + \left(1 - \dfrac{\sqrt{35}}{2}\right)}

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Understanding the Problem**: We are to find the equation of a parabola with vertex at (0,3) and directrix y = 3.

2. **Recalling Parabola Formulas**:
   - For vertical parabolas: \( x^2 = 4p(y - k) \), where the vertex is at (h,k). Directrix is \( y = k - p \).
   - For horizontal parabolas: \( y^2 = 4p(x - h) \), with directrix \( x = h - p \).

3. **Applying to Given Data**:
   - Vertex at (0,3), so h=0 and k=3.
   - Directrix given as y=3.

4. **Vertical Parabola Case**:
   - Using vertical parabola formula: \( x^2 = 4p(y - 3) \).
   - Directrix equation for this case is \( y = 3 - p \). Setting it equal to the given directrix y=3 gives \( 3 - p = 3 \), so p=0.
   - Since p must be non-zero for a parabola, this is impossible.

5. **Horizontal Parabola Case**:
   - Using horizontal parabola formula: \( y^2 = 4p(x) \).
   - Directrix equation here would be \( x = 0 - p \), which is invalid because the directrix given is a horizontal line, not vertical.
   
6. **Conclusion**: In both cases, either p=0 (which isn't valid) or conditions contradict each other.

Answer: No such parabola exists under these conditions.

- \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.
- The focus is at \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \).
- The directrix is the line \( y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \).

- \( h = 1 \)
- \( k = 5 \)
- \( a = 1 \)

\( y = 5 - \frac{1}{4(1)} = 4.75 \)

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). Hmm, let's see. Since it's a parabola, its general form is y = ax² + bx + c. My goal is to find the values of a, b, and c that satisfy all three points.

First, I'll plug in each point into the equation to create a system of equations. Let's start with (0, 4) because it looks like it might be simpler since x is zero there.

So, for (0,4): y = a*(0)^2 + b*(0) + c → 4 = 0 + 0 + c → c = 4. Great, so now I know c is 4. That simplifies the equation to y = ax² + bx + 4.

Next, let's use the point (2,8). Plugging in x=2 and y=8: 8 = a*(2)^2 + b*2 + 4 → 8 = 4a + 2b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides gives 4 = 4a + 2b.

Similarly, for the point (-1,3): x=-1 and y=3. So, 3 = a*(-1)^2 + b*(-1) + 4 → 3 = a - b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides gives -1 = a - b.

Now I have two equations: 

1) 4a + 2b = 4
2) a - b = -1

I can solve this system of equations to find a and b. Let's rewrite equation 2 as a = b -1.

Substitute a in equation 1 with (b-1):

4*(b -1) + 2b = 4 → 4b -4 + 2b = 4 → 6b -4 = 4 → 6b = 8 → b = 8/6 = 4/3.

Now that I have b, plug it back into equation 2 to find a:

a = (4/3) -1 = 1/3.

So now I have a=1/3, b=4/3, c=4. Therefore, the equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.
</think>

The equation of the parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) can be found using these steps:

1. **General Form**: Start with the general form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \).

2. **Plug in Points**:
   - For (0,4): \( 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \) gives \( c = 4 \).
   - For (2,8): \( 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \), simplifying to \( 4a + 2b = 4 \).
   - For (-1,3): \( 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \), simplifying to \( a - b = -1 \).

3. **Solve the System**:
   - From the second equation: \( a = b - 1 \).
   - Substitute into the first equation: \( 4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \) → \( 6b - 4 = 4 \) → \( 6b = 8 \) → \( b = \frac{4}{3} \).
   - Then, \( a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3} \).

4. **Equation**:
   Substituting back: \( y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \).

Final Answer: {Solution: "y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4"}

- The distance p from the vertex to the focus (and also to the directrix) is calculated by finding the absolute difference between the y-coordinates of the vertex and the directrix. Thus, \( |p| = |-4 - (-1)| = 3 \). Since it's a vertical parabola opening upward, p is positive.

Given the problem of finding the equation of a parabola with vertex at \((-2, 1)\) that passes through the point \((-4, 1)\), we start by considering both horizontal and vertical orientations.

1. **Horizontal Parabola**:
   - The general form is \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\).
   - Substituting the vertex \((-2, 1)\), the equation becomes \((y - 1)^2 = 4p(x + 2)\).
   - Plugging in the point \((-4, 1)\):
     \[
     (1 - 1)^2 = 4p(-4 + 2) \implies 0 = 4p(-2) \implies p = 0
     \]
   - This results in a degenerate parabola, which is the line \(y = 1\).

2. **Vertical Parabola**:
   - The general form is \((x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)\).
   - Substituting the vertex \((-2, 1)\), the equation becomes \((x + 2)^2 = 4p(y - 1)\).
   - Plugging in the point \((-4, 1)\):
     \[
     (-4 + 2)^2 = 4p(1 - 1) \implies 4 = 0
     \]
   - This is a contradiction, indicating no solution for a vertical parabola.

Given the above analysis, the only feasible solution that satisfies both conditions (vertex at \((-2, 1)\) and passing through \((-4, 1)\)) is the degenerate case where the equation simplifies to \(y = 1\).

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola is \boxed{y = 1}.

The given equation \( X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle's equation by completing the square.

1. Group terms:
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X) + (Y^2 - 4Y) = 12
   \]
   
2. Complete the square for X and Y:
   - For \( X^2 + 6X \), add and subtract \( (6/2)^2 = 9 \).
   - For \( Y^2 - 4Y \), add and subtract \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \).

3. Rewrite the equation:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 - 9 + (Y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
   \]
   
4. Simplify:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

From the standard form \( (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \), we find:
- Center: (-3, 2)
- Radius: √25 = 5

The equation of the circle with its center at the origin (0, 0) is derived as follows:

1. The general equation of a circle is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.
2. Since the center is at the origin, substitute \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\): 
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]
3. The circle passes through the point (0, 5). Substitute \(x = 0\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation to find \(r^2\):
   \[
   0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]
4. Therefore, \(r = 5\) (since radius cannot be negative).
5. The equation of the circle is:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]

The standard form of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a major axis length of 10 and minor axis length of 6 is determined as follows:

1. Calculate the semi-major axis \(a\) by dividing the major axis length by 2: 
   \[
   a = \frac{10}{2} = 5
   \]
2. Calculate the semi-minor axis \(b\) similarly:
   \[
   b = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]
3. Assuming the major axis is along the x-axis, the equation of the ellipse is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
4. Substituting \(a\) and \(b\):
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

1. **Identify Parameters**:
   - \(a^2 = 25\) ⇒ \(a = 5\)
   - \(b^2 = 16\) ⇒ \(b = 4\)

2. **Determine the Major Axis**:
   - Since \(a > b\), the major axis is along the x-axis.

3. **Calculate Foci Distance (c)**:

- Foci: (3, 0), (-3, 0)
- Vertices: (5, 0), (-5, 0)

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

- \(a\) (distance from center to vertex) = 4 → \(a^2 = 16\)
- \(c\) (distance from center to focus) = 6

Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\):

\[
36 = 16 + b^2 \implies b^2 = 20
\]

Thus, the equation is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{20} = 1
\]

The asymptotes of a hyperbola are the lines that the hyperbola approaches but never touches. For a hyperbola in this standard form, the equations of the asymptotes are y = (b/a)x and y = -(b/a)x. Plugging in the values I have: b=4 and a=3. So, the slopes are 4/3 and -4/3.

Therefore, the equations of the asymptotes should be y = (4/3)x and y = -(4/3)x.

Now, for the foci. The distance from the center to each focus is given by c, where c² = a² + b². Calculating that: c² = 9 + 16 = 25, so c = sqrt(25) = 5.

To find the asymptotes and foci of the hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \):

**Step 1: Identify a² and b²**
- \( a^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( a = 3 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

**Step 2: Calculate Asymptotes**
The asymptotes for this hyperbola are:
\[ y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \]

**Step 3: Calculate Foci**
Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \):
- \( c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

The foci are located at:
\[ (c, 0) = (5, 0) \]
and
\[ (-c, 0) = (-5, 0) \]

The problem requires finding the equation of a vertical hyperbola passing through the point (2,3) with \(a = 3\) and \(b = 4\). However, without additional information such as another point or the location of the center, it's impossible to determine a unique equation. There are infinitely many hyperbolas that satisfy these conditions, each with a different center \((h, k)\).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **General Form of a Vertical Hyperbola**:
   The standard form is \(\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \((h, k)\) is the center.

2. **Plugging in Known Values**:
   Substituting \(a = 3\) and \(b = 4\):
   \[
   \frac{(y - k)^2}{9} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{16} = 1
   \]
   
3. **Using the Given Point (2,3)**:
   Plugging in \(x = 2\) and \(y = 3\):
   \[
   \frac{(3 - k)^2}{9} - \frac{(2 - h)^2}{16} = 1
   \]
   
4. **Solving for the Relationship Between h and k**:
   Multiplying both sides by 144 (LCM of 9 and 16) to eliminate denominators:
   \[
   16(3 - k)^2 - 9(2 - h)^2 = 144
   \]
   
5. **Expanding and Simplifying**:
   Expanding the terms leads to a quadratic equation in \(h\) and \(k\):
   \[
   16k^2 - 96k + 36h - 9h^2 - 36 = 0
   \]
   
6. **Conclusion**:
   Since this is a quadratic equation with two variables, there are infinitely many solutions for \(h\) and \(k\), meaning multiple hyperbolas pass through (2,3) without additional constraints.

Answer: It's not possible to determine a unique hyperbola with the given information; there are infinitely many such hyperbolas.

To find the equation of the hyperbola with asymptotes having slopes ±2, we consider two cases: one where the transverse axis is horizontal and another where it is vertical.

1. **Horizontal Transverse Axis**:
   - The standard form is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\).
   - The slopes of the asymptotes are given by \( \pm \frac{b}{a} \). Since the slopes are ±2, we have \( \frac{b}{a} = 2 \) or \( b = 2a \).
   - Substituting \( b = 2a \), the equation becomes:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} = 1 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{4a^2} = 1
     \]

2. **Vertical Transverse Axis**:
   - The standard form is \( \frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1 \).
   - The slopes of the asymptotes are given by \( \pm \frac{b}{a} \). Since the slopes are ±2, we have \( \frac{b}{a} = 2 \) or \( b = 2a \).
   - Substituting \( b = 2a \), the equation becomes:
     \[
     \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \frac{y^2}{4a^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1
     \]

Since the problem does not specify whether the transverse axis is horizontal or vertical, both cases are possible. However, typically, without additional information, we often consider one case. For the sake of a single answer, we will present the equation assuming the **horizontal transverse axis**.

\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{4a^2} = 1}
\]

The hyperbola has its vertices at (0, ±3) and foci at (0, ±5). Since both are on the y-axis, it's a vertical hyperbola with standard form \(\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\).

Given:
- \(a = 3\) (distance from center to vertex)
- \(c = 5\) (distance from center to focus)

Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\):
\(5^2 = 3^2 + b^2\)
\(25 = 9 + b^2\)
\(b^2 = 16\)
So, \(b = 4\).

The equation of the hyperbola is:
\(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1\)
